# Contributor guide — Invariant tests

> **What this guide is.** A pattern catalogue for the
> `tests/test_*_invariant_*.py` family that this repository uses
> heavily. Read this before adding a new feature that needs
> long-term protection against silent decay; read this before
> _deleting_ an invariant test (so you understand what you're
> giving up); read this if you're confused why CI just failed
> on a test that looks like it tests nothing runtime-y.

> **What this guide is not.** A general testing tutorial. For
> classic unit / integration / end-to-end discipline see
> [`docs/lessons-learned-silent-decay.md`](lessons-learned-silent-decay.md)
> and the existing test files.

> **简体中文版本**: [`contributor-guide-invariant-tests.zh-CN.md`](contributor-guide-invariant-tests.zh-CN.md).

## 1. What is an invariant test?

An **invariant test** asserts a structural property of the codebase
or its outputs that **must remain true across refactors**. It is
not a behavioural test (which asserts what the code _does_ at
runtime). It is a contract test for the codebase shape itself.

Three example invariants that this repo currently locks:

- **R220 / R224**: every `aiia_*` metric name referenced inside
  `docs/observability/grafana-dashboard*.json` must substring-
  exist in `src/ai_intervention_agent/web_ui_routes/system.py`.
  Without this, renaming a metric in `system.py` silently breaks
  the imported Grafana dashboard, and ops doesn't notice until
  the panel goes blank.
- **R217**: `src/ai_intervention_agent/static/js/state.js` and
  `packages/vscode/webview-state.js` must be **byte-identical**.
  Without this, a bug fix in one file silently leaves the other
  carrying the original bug.
- **R215**: the `needed` tuple in `scripts/smoke_test_r50.py`
  must list every scalar key in the `SSEBusStatsSnapshot`
  TypedDict in `task.py`. Without this, adding a new SSE
  observability field silently fails to verify in the production
  smoke test.

Invariant tests are cheap (most run in milliseconds) but pay
back **every refactor**, **every code review**, and **every
contributor onboarding**.

## 2. When to write one — decision tree

Before adding a feature, ask:

1. **Will the feature's correctness depend on multiple files
   staying in sync?**
   - If yes → invariant test almost certainly warranted.
   - If no → maybe not; behavioural tests suffice.
2. **Is the sync rule something a code review would catch reliably?**
   - "All metric names must match between dashboard JSON and
     Python source" — a reviewer would miss this 1 time in 10.
     **Write the invariant.**
   - "`max_attempts` must be a positive integer" — a reviewer
     catches this 100% of the time. Don't bother.
3. **Will the feature outlive its current author?**
   - Yes (almost certainly, in any healthy project) → write the
     invariant so the next contributor doesn't accidentally
     unwind the design decision.
4. **Is the failure mode silent?**
   - "Grafana panel renders blank" — silent. **Write the invariant.**
   - "App crashes immediately on startup" — loud. Don't bother;
     normal CI catches it.
5. **Is the cost of the failure higher than the cost of the test?**
   - Tests cost ~30 minutes to write + maintain.
   - Failures cost hours-to-days of debugging + a user-visible
     outage. **Almost always: write the invariant.**

## 3. Five recurring patterns

This repo has accumulated **12+ invariant tests across 12 R-cycles**
(R212, R213, R215, R216, R217, R219, R220, R221, R222, R223, R225,
R226). They cluster into five reusable patterns.

### Pattern A — Static-source string-presence check

**Use case**: lock a piece of code to "never contain X" or
"always contain X".

**Example**: `tests/test_notification_manager_console_noise_invariant_r216.py`
asserts `src/ai_intervention_agent/static/js/notification-manager.js`
contains **zero** `console.log(` calls (they should be
`console.debug(` for production-quiet operation).

**Recipe**:

```python
from pathlib import Path

REPO_ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
TARGET = REPO_ROOT / "src" / "..." / "your_file.js"


def test_no_console_log_calls() -> None:
    source = TARGET.read_text(encoding="utf-8")
    count = source.count("console.log(")
    assert count == 0, (
        f"{TARGET.name} contains {count} console.log(...) call(s); "
        "should be console.debug(...) for production-quiet operation."
    )
```

**When to upgrade** to an AST scan (Pattern B):

- The string is also a substring of unrelated comments / docstrings
  that would cause false positives.
- You need to count call-sites accurately (`console.log(` inside a
  string literal would otherwise count).
- You need to enforce "every call to X has a corresponding call
  to Y" (cross-call invariants).

### Pattern B — AST-based call-site enumeration

**Use case**: count or constrain function calls / class
references at a structural level.

**Example**: `tests/test_sse_event_schemas_r198.py` walks every
`*.py` file under `src/`, parses with `ast.parse`, finds every
`_sse_bus.emit("<literal>", ...)` call, and asserts each literal
event type appears in the `KNOWN_SSE_EVENTS` registry of
`sse_event_schemas.py`.

**Recipe**:

```python
import ast
from pathlib import Path

def _emit_event_types(path: Path) -> set[str]:
    tree = ast.parse(path.read_text(encoding="utf-8"))
    found: set[str] = set()
    for node in ast.walk(tree):
        if (
            isinstance(node, ast.Call)
            and isinstance(node.func, ast.Attribute)
            and node.func.attr == "emit"
            and node.args
            and isinstance(node.args[0], ast.Constant)
            and isinstance(node.args[0].value, str)
        ):
            found.add(node.args[0].value)
    return found
```

**Why AST over regex**: regex matches the string `"emit"` inside
comments, doc strings, and other functions named `emit`. AST sees
only real method invocations.

### Pattern C — JSON / YAML structural check

**Use case**: lock the structure of a config / data file
shipped with the project.

**Example**: `tests/test_grafana_dashboard_invariant_r220.py`
parses `docs/observability/grafana-dashboard.json`, asserts:

- `schemaVersion` is in the supported Grafana 10–11 range
- `uid` is the stable `aiia-overview-r220` value
- `panels` count is exactly 7 (changes to layout are deliberate
  edits, not accidents)
- every panel has a non-empty unique title
- every `aiia_*` metric in panel targets exists in `system.py`

**Recipe**:

```python
import json
from pathlib import Path

DASHBOARD = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent / "docs" / "..."


def test_dashboard_structure() -> None:
    data = json.loads(DASHBOARD.read_text(encoding="utf-8"))
    panels = data.get("panels") or []
    assert len(panels) == 7, (
        f"Panel count drifted: expected 7, got {len(panels)}. "
        "If you intentionally added/removed panels, also update "
        "this test's expected count."
    )
    titles = [p.get("title") for p in panels]
    assert len(titles) == len(set(titles)), "Duplicate panel titles"
```

**Pro tip**: when locking a count (panels, fields, options), add
a comment in the test that says "if you intentionally change
this, also update the expected count". Without it, the next
contributor adding a panel will spend 10 minutes wondering why
their commit failed CI.

### Pattern D — Bilingual locale parity

**Use case**: the project ships `en.json` + `zh-CN.json`; new
keys must be present in both, message lengths must be in a
reasonable ratio range (catches accidental empty translations).

**Example**: `tests/test_notification_fallback_toast_invariant_r214.py`
asserts every `status.notifFallback*` key in `en.json` has a
matching key in `zh-CN.json`, and the Chinese length is within
[0.4, 2.5]× the English length (catches truncated translations
and runaway-verbose ones).

**Recipe**:

```python
import json
from pathlib import Path

LOCALES = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent / "src" / "..." / "static" / "locales"


def test_bilingual_key_parity() -> None:
    en = json.loads((LOCALES / "en.json").read_text())
    zh = json.loads((LOCALES / "zh-CN.json").read_text())

    def flatten(prefix: str, obj: dict) -> dict[str, str]:
        result: dict[str, str] = {}
        for k, v in obj.items():
            full_key = f"{prefix}.{k}" if prefix else k
            if isinstance(v, dict):
                result.update(flatten(full_key, v))
            elif isinstance(v, str):
                result[full_key] = v
        return result

    en_keys = flatten("", en)
    zh_keys = flatten("", zh)
    missing_in_zh = set(en_keys) - set(zh_keys)
    assert not missing_in_zh, f"zh-CN missing keys: {sorted(missing_in_zh)}"
```

**Don't forget**: when adding new i18n keys, also run
`uv run python scripts/gen_pseudo_locale.py` to regenerate the
`_pseudo/pseudo.json` locale that other tests check.

### Pattern E — Cross-tool / cross-file byte parity

**Use case**: two files must be **byte-identical** because they
are meant to be the "same" code in different distribution
contexts (e.g. shared logic between Web UI and VS Code webview).

**Example**: `tests/test_state_machine.py::TestJsSync::test_two_js_files_are_byte_identical`
asserts `src/ai_intervention_agent/static/js/state.js` and
`packages/vscode/webview-state.js` have identical bytes. The
files are intentionally duplicated rather than imported because
the VS Code webview cannot reach files outside `packages/vscode`
without inflating the .vsix.

**Recipe**:

```python
import hashlib
from pathlib import Path

TWIN_A = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent / "src" / "..."
TWIN_B = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent / "packages" / "..."


def test_twins_are_byte_identical() -> None:
    a_hash = hashlib.sha256(TWIN_A.read_bytes()).hexdigest()
    b_hash = hashlib.sha256(TWIN_B.read_bytes()).hexdigest()
    assert a_hash == b_hash, (
        f"{TWIN_A.relative_to(TWIN_A.parents[3])} and "
        f"{TWIN_B.relative_to(TWIN_A.parents[3])} are no longer "
        "byte-identical. If you fixed a bug in one, copy the same "
        "fix to the other; the duplication is deliberate because "
        "the VS Code webview cannot import files outside packages/vscode."
    )
```

**Mid-cycle drift R217 caught**: demoting `console.log` to
`console.debug` in `state.js` without applying the same
change in `webview-state.js` broke this test mid-PR; one minute
to fix vs hours of "why does this work in browser but not in
VS Code?" debugging later.

## 4. Anti-patterns to avoid

- **Don't write an invariant that duplicates a behaviour test.**
  If `test_handler_returns_200_on_valid_input` already passes
  whenever the handler is sane, you don't also need
  `test_handler_function_exists`.

- **Don't lock a count that is naturally going to grow.**
  `assert len(items) == 7` is good for "panel count of a
  finished dashboard". `assert len(items) == 7` is **bad** for
  "number of supported MCP tools" — that grows.
  Use `assert len(items) >= 7` for "grows monotonically" or
  `assert 7 <= len(items) <= 50` for "stays within a sane band".

- **Don't read the same file three times in three tests.**
  Cache the parse result in a module-level constant or a
  `setUpModule()` so 30 invariants don't compound 30 ASTs.

- **Don't write an invariant test that doesn't say WHY in its
  docstring.**
  Future you will not remember why "metric name parity" matters
  6 months from now. Write the docstring as if explaining to a
  contributor who just landed and asked "can I delete this test
  please, it's failing my PR".

- **Don't make the invariant test require ground-truth data
  that doesn't exist in the repo.**
  If your test needs the production Prometheus instance to
  scrape metrics, it's a production smoke test, not an invariant
  test. Move it to `scripts/smoke_test_*.py` and call from a
  dedicated CI job instead.

## 5. Workflow

1. Decide an invariant is warranted (use §2 decision tree).
2. Pick the most relevant pattern from §3.
3. Create `tests/test_<feature>_invariant_r<NNN>.py` named
   after the R-cycle landing the invariant.
4. Open with a multi-line module docstring explaining:
   - which R-cycle added this and why
   - what specific drift the test prevents
   - links to the CR / CHANGELOG entry that motivates it
   - the cases the test covers (numbered list)
5. Write the test using one of the recipes above. Prefer
   `unittest.TestCase` subclasses (matches the rest of the repo).
6. Run `uv run pytest tests/test_<feature>_invariant_r<NNN>.py`
   to confirm green on the current tree.
7. Deliberately break the invariant in your dev branch (e.g.
   rename a metric, add a missing key) and confirm the test
   **fails** with an actionable message. **If the message is
   not actionable, rewrite it.**
8. Restore the working tree, commit the test along with the
   feature it protects, and add a CHANGELOG entry citing the
   R-cycle number.

| R712    | `tests/test_lottie_plays_despite_reduced_motion_r712.py`                 | Pattern C + product contract | **Empty-state animations exempt from Reduce Motion (supersedes the R704 rest-frame contract)**. R704 parked the sprout on a rest frame for `prefers-reduced-motion` users; on real devices that read as "the animation is broken" (the loading bar was simultaneously flattened to a one-shot 0.01ms run by the global reduce rule). R712 locks the maintainer's on-device decision: the empty-state Lottie sprout runs `autoplay: true` unconditionally, the R704 preference machinery (watcher / rest-frame helper) is fully removed, and main.css keeps the `.no-content-progress-dot` exemption (`animation-iteration-count: infinite !important`; the class was `.no-content-progress-bar` until R894 replaced the bar with three dots, and the contract did not change) inside the global reduce block; the `error`-handler destroy-before-fallback ordering contract carries over from R704 unchanged. Every other animation keeps honoring the system preference.                            |
| R713    | `tests/test_close_terminal_state_r713.py`                                | Pattern C + i18n keys       | **"Close Web UI" terminal-card guard**. The old flow force-reloaded 2s after `/api/close`, but the server shuts down 0.5s in — the reload could only land on the SW's offline.html ("cannot connect" + a forever-useless Retry button) or the browser's native error page, even though the user closed the UI on purpose. Locks: the success branch calls `renderClosedTerminalState()` to render an in-place success-semantics card and `return`s (never reloads) while the failure branch keeps the refresh fallback; the renderer must stop SSE (full `_disconnectSSE` cleanup), dispose the Lottie lifecycle, swap in `status.closedTitle/closedHint` (stripping `data-i18n` so a late translateDOM can't overwrite — the R709 lesson), and hide the progress bar / close button / SSE badge; all three locales must ship the terminal keys with `closedRefreshing` removed alongside the behavior.                            |
| R714    | `tests/test_notification_electron_webview_fallback_r714.py`              | Pattern C               | **Electron-webview notification fallback guard** (Cursor / VS Code built-in browser). In Electron webviews, service-worker `showNotification()` commonly *silently fails* — the promise resolves but no notification is shown (electron#13041 / #10146); the old code treated the resolve as success and never tried the page-level `new Notification()`, so notifications were lost. Locks the two defense layers in `showSystemNotification`: (1) `isElectronHost()` UA probe (`Electron/` token) skips the SW path entirely and goes straight to the page-level constructor (which Electron maps to native notifications); (2) after a SW `showNotification` resolves, `getNotifications({tag})` verifies the notification actually registered — an empty result falls back to the page-level path, while a query *exception* is conservatively treated as success so SW-only platforms (Android Chrome, where the page constructor throws) keep their behavior.                            |

| R731    | `tests/test_multi_task_composer_isolation_r731.py`                       | Pattern C + Node runtime           | **The feedback form belongs to exactly one task at a time**. Switching to a task that had no draft showed the previous task's text and attached images, stored that text as the new task's draft (it survived a reload) and sent the images with the new task's feedback; when the poll rather than a click moved the active task (another client activated one, or the task being written in was finished or closed elsewhere) nothing was saved and the images were lost; the restore ran only after the details request, so a slow or failed request left the wrong text on screen and a keystroke then overwrote the parked draft. Locks, with the shipped `multi_task.js` run in a Node `vm` against a small DOM model: every change of `activeTaskId` goes through `setActiveTaskId`, which parks the leaving task (text, options, images) only while it is still open, empties the form and loads the entering task's parked state at once; a switch decided by the poll behaves like a click; a finished, closed or vanished task leaves nothing behind and is never parked again; the form is emptied when no task is left; text and images present before any task owned the form are kept at the first activation while a saved draft wins; option states are saved only when the rendered options belong to that task (a quick A to B to C never copies A's checkboxes into B); the images are cloned and their object URLs are never revoked on the way out, so the parked previews stay valid; `loadTaskDetails` no longer writes the text box or the images; choosing the task that is already active changes nothing; the character counter is refreshed exactly when the form changes; a form that cannot be read or filled is logged and never blocks the task switch. Mutation-checked with 16 mutants, all killed (parking by reference is caught by the static pin of R572). Also verified in a real Chromium against a real server (text, images, options, reload, close, rapid A-B-C, first activation). |
| R732    | `tests/test_modal_inert_nested_dialog_r732.py`                           | Pattern C + Node runtime           | **An open dialog is never inside the inert subtree, wherever it sits**. `inert` is inherited, and the helper that blanks the page behind a dialog marked only the direct children of `.container` except the dialog; the paste-code dialog sits two levels deeper, so its ancestor was made inert and with it the dialog itself: focus stayed on the page, typing did nothing and Insert could not be clicked (the source-text checks of R244 could not see it). Locks, running the shipped helpers of `app.js` and `settings-manager.js` against a DOM built from the real `templates/web_ui.html` and against a synthetic deeply nested tree: for every `role="dialog"` the dialog, everything above it and everything inside it stay interactive, everything else under `.container` is inert, a dialog outside `.container` still blanks all of it, and closing leaves nothing inert. Mutation-checked with 9 mutants (the original direct-children behaviour in either helper, a one-level walk, marking the path nodes, not skipping the path, ignoring the value, dropping the outside-container fallback), all killed. Also verified in a real Chromium: before, focus stayed on `<body>` and neither typing nor clicking did anything; after, the dialog takes focus, accepts text, inserts the code block and closes, and nothing stays inert. |
| R733    | `tests/test_keyboard_shortcuts_tab_native_r733.py`                       | Pattern C + Node runtime           | **The Tab shortcut never takes the Tab key away from the browser**. The `Tab` / `Shift+Tab` task-switch shortcut was registered with the registry's default `preventDefault: true`, so the key was swallowed before the handler looked at the tabs: with one task (or none) focus could not move from `<body>`, a button or a link, and while a dialog was open the dialog's focus trap could not move focus (it stuck on the Cancel button) and, with several tasks, every press switched the task behind the dialog. Locks, running the shipped `keyboard-shortcuts.js` in a Node `vm`: with zero or one task tab `Tab` and `Shift+Tab` are left to the browser for a button, a link and the page background; with several tabs they still switch the task and prevent the default; while a modal dialog is open they do neither; inputs and modified Tab keys are untouched; the open-dialog query excludes both closed forms (`hidden` attribute and `hidden` class); and every `aria-modal` element of the real template starts out closed in one of those forms, so the shortcut is not disabled from the first paint. Mutation-checked with 10 mutants (default `preventDefault` back on either registration, a missing guard on either key, a query that never or always matches, a query that treats either closed form as open, switching without preventing the default), all killed. Also verified in a real Chromium (20 checks): Tab walks the page with one task, cycles through all four controls of the paste dialog and through the settings dialog, never switches the task behind the paste, settings, image and help dialogs (counting real task-tab clicks), and the shortcut works again once they close. |
| R734    | `tests/test_programmatic_textarea_writes_notify_r734.py`                 | Pattern C + Node runtime           | **Programmatic writes to the feedback box reach the listeners that mirror it**. The character counter, the stored draft and the in-memory composer only learn about edits from `input` events, but two `app.js` paths assigned `textarea.value` silently: inserting a code block (paste dialog and clipboard shortcut) left the counter on the old length and both drafts on the text from before the insert (and did not count as typing for the auto-submit hold), and the success path of `submitFeedback` left the counter showing the length of the text that had just been sent whenever the follow-up list refresh failed or returned the same task. Locks, running the real `app.js` functions in a Node harness: a code insert fires exactly one bubbling `input` event, after the value and the caret are final, for the empty box, a caret at the end, a caret mid-line and a body containing backtick runs; blank text or a missing box dispatches nothing; the real `feedback_char_counter.js` follows an insert end to end (and is probed to stay stale after a silent write); a successful submit refreshes the counter exactly once, after the clear, without dispatching a synthetic `input` (which would stamp the typing marker and hold the next task's countdown); a submit that succeeded for another task or that failed leaves the counter alone; a missing counter helper does not break the submit. Mutation-checked with 12 mutants (no event, event too early, `change` instead of `input`, no bubbling, double event, missing, early, doubled or stale-task refresh, a synthetic `input` instead of the refresh, an unguarded helper call), all killed. Also verified in a real Chromium (12 checks, 7 of them failing before the fix): counter, in-memory draft and stored draft follow a dialog insert and a clipboard insert, the insert counts as typing activity, the counter is hidden after a submit whose list refresh fails, and a submit does not stamp the typing marker. |
| R735    | `tests/test_image_modal_close_button_csp_r735.py`                        | Pattern C + Node runtime           | **The image preview's × button closes it without an inline event handler**. The Web UI's `script-src` allows no inline scripts, so the one remaining inline `onclick="closeImageModal()"` (on the × button of the image preview) was never executed: Chromium logged a CSP violation on every page load, and clicking the button, or pressing Enter or Space on it, left the preview open (only `Esc` and a backdrop click worked). Locks: the real template has no inline event-handler attribute (`on*`) on any element and its close button is a labelled `type="button"` inside the dialog; running the real `image-upload.js` in a Node `vm` with a DOM that dispatches and bubbles events, a click on the × button closes the dialog and hands focus back to the opener (keyboard handlers detached), each close path (button, backdrop) runs the close logic exactly once while a click on the picture or its container keeps the dialog open, and repeated initialisation wires each listener once (also without a button in the template). Mutation-checked with 8 mutants (button never wired, any click inside closing, init guard or flag lost, `mousedown` instead of `click`, backdrop wiring lost, inline handler back in the template, double close), all killed. Also verified in a real Chromium (11 checks; the CSP error and the three button checks failed before): no CSP violation during the whole session, the × button closes the preview by mouse, Enter and Space, `Esc` and the backdrop still work, a click on the picture keeps it open, and repeated open/close cycles end closed. |
| R736    | `tests/test_image_upload_live_filelist_r736.py`                          | Pattern C + Node runtime           | **Picking several images at once attaches every one of them**. The file input's `change` handler resets the input right after `handleFileUpload(target.files)` starts, and the browser empties that live `FileList` when it does; `handleFileUpload` reads the list again for every batch of three, so with more than three images picked only the first three were attached (the rest failed with "invalid file object"), and the one-file summary lost its name. The older tests fed it plain arrays and plain objects that never change. A second defect lived in the same summary: when the only picked file was rejected, the specific reason that `addImageToList` had just shown was replaced by a bare "failed". Locks, running the real `image-upload.js` in a Node `vm` with a `FileList` stand-in that empties itself like the real one: seven files picked at once are all handed to `addImageToList` in order across three batches and summarised as 7/7; a single picked file is still named in the success message; a single rejected file gets a summary that carries the file name and repeats the reason that was shown; a cancelled upload (the image was removed while it was processed) stays silent and never reuses an earlier reason; a null or empty selection is a quiet no-op. Mutation-checked with 10 mutants plus a full revert (no snapshot, a snapshot that drops the last file or keeps only the first batch, no null guard, the reason dropped, never remembered or not reset, a failure summary for a cancelled upload, bypassed bookkeeping, the wrong file name), all killed. Also verified in a real Chromium (9 checks, 6 of them failing before the fix): 4, 5, 7 and 12 files picked at once attach 4, 5, 7 and 10 (the limit) files, five dropped files attach five, the success message names the file, and a rejected file's message carries its name and the reason. |
| R737    | `tests/test_zhtw_locales_no_simplified_chars_r737.py`                    | Pattern A + data invariant         | **The zh-TW text the project ships contains no Simplified-only characters**. `zh-TW.json` was derived from `zh-CN.json` by a generator with a partial character table and then edited by hand, and nothing checked the result beyond a few spot checks: 18 Web UI strings and 9 extension strings kept Simplified characters (`请换一個檔案`, `解码`, `路径`, `帮助`, `详情`, `診断`, `日志` ...), and the generator's phrase `未授予 -> 尚未授予` had turned `尚未授予` into `尚尚未授予`. Locks, for both `zh-TW.json` files, the server's zh-TW message table and the offline page's inline zh-TW block: no ideograph that Python's `big5` codec (Taiwan's Traditional set) refuses, none of a short curated list of Simplified forms Big5 still encodes (`触 坏 万 与 机` ...), none of a short list of Simplified words spelled with shared characters (`日志`, `复制`, `然后` ...), and no doubled ideograph that the zh-CN twin does not also have (`谢谢` -> `謝謝` stays legal). The detector has its own table tests (Simplified flagged; `只 台 后 里 干 面` and real Traditional text spared), a non-vacuity check (zh-CN must light up with at least 150 distinct characters), hygiene checks on the curated lists, a check that all four sources are really read, and unit tests of the doubled-character helper. Mutation-checked with 21 mutants (a Simplified character, phrase or doubled character put back into each source, and ten detector mutations), all killed. Also verified that the running Web UI serves the corrected file identically in identity, gzip and Brotli encodings. |
| R738    | `tests/test_zhtw_generator_merge_r738.py`                                 | Pattern A + data invariant         | **The zh-TW generator keeps what is already translated and derives clean text**. The release checklist ran `scripts/gen_zhtw_from_zhcn.py --all`, which re-derived every zh-TW string and overwrote the files (the 14 Web UI strings worded by hand would have been lost, and the Simplified characters its table lacks would have come back), and the derivation itself was unsafe: phrases were replaced one after another over the whole text, so a phrase's output could be rewritten again (`尚未授予` became `尚尚未授予`); the one-character phrases `块 -> 區塊` and `段 -> 區段` fired inside other words and shipped as `模區塊` (3 VS Code strings) and `一區段程式碼`; and 40 characters of the shipped zh-CN text had no table entry. Mutation-checked with 37 mutants (generator code, table entries, shipped text, checklist), each killed by the test meant for it. |
| R739    | `tests/test_zhtw_generator_tables_r739.py`                                | Pattern A + data invariant         | **The zh-TW generator's per-character table holds only entries that can match**. `CHAR_MAP_v2` is read one character at a time, yet 232 of its 556 entries had two to four characters (`实际`, `贡献者`, `赖账不还`...) and could never match; behind them the table lacked 35 single characters those words need (`际`, `业`, `状`, `账`...), so a new zh-CN string with `实际` came out as `實际`, and the spellings that depend on the word (`赞助` is `贊助`, `细致` is `細緻`, while `一致` keeps its `致`) were never applied. Locks: every table key is one character; every entry turns one character into a different one; every target can be encoded as Big5 (a Traditional form, not a leftover or a typo); a character is defined in one table only; the 43 words the dead entries described convert as intended; the 35 carried-over characters convert; words with two spellings are not forced into one (`一致`, `致辞`, `致谢`, `赞美`, `数据`); a sentence mixing the new characters is converted whole. A derivation of both zh-CN files is byte for byte what it was before. Mutation-checked with 15 mutants (words back in the table, identity and Simplified targets, a forced `致 -> 緻`, dropped phrases and characters, wrong variants, a one-character phrase shadowing the table), each killed by the test meant for it. |
| R740    | `tests/test_notification_skip_unavailable_r740.py`                        | Pattern A + runtime                 | **A notification channel with no provider is skipped, not retried as a failed delivery**. For every feedback request the MCP server asks for the system, sound and Bark channels; a channel has a provider only when its switch is on and, for system notifications, when `plyer` (plus `pyobjus` on macOS) can be used. With none of the three available the manager counted "no provider" as a failed delivery: three retries two seconds apart, then the fallback, i.e. four WARNING lines and about seven seconds of timers per task, although nothing could ever succeed. Locks: `send_notification(..., skip_unavailable=True)` creates no event when no requested channel has a provider (returns `""`, no queue entry, no statistics, no WARNING); sends only to the channels that do have one; leaves the caller's list alone; the default keeps the old accounting (`provider_not_registered`, the "all channels failed" WARNING) that the health views read (R143 / R145); a registered provider that fails is still a failure; a `system_enabled = true` that cannot work is reported once when the providers are created, and not when the switch is off or the channel works. The two MCP call sites passing the flag is asserted next to their other notification checks in `tests/test_server_functions.py`. Mutation-checked with 10 mutants (flag ignored, flag defaulting on, inverted filter, an id returned for nothing, an event without channels, the skip logged as a warning, the flag dropped at either call site, the warning removed or also fired when the switch is off), each killed by the test meant for it. |
| R741    | `tests/test_vscode_webview_protocol_relative_url_r741.py`                 | Pattern C + Node runtime            | **A protocol-relative URL in a prompt keeps its own host in the VS Code webview**. The panel's sanitiser puts the Web UI server in front of every URL that starts with `/` (R726); the test was `startsWith('/')`, which also matched `//host/path`, so `[docs](//example.com/docs)` became `http://127.0.0.1:8080//example.com/docs`, a 404 on the local server instead of the page on `example.com`. Locks, running the real `normalizeUrl` from `webview-ui.js` in Node against an http and an https server: `//host/path` (with a port, a query and a fragment) resolves to the server's scheme and keeps its host; a path with one leading slash (`/a.png`, `/api/tasks/t-1/images/0`, `/x//y`, `/`) still gets the server in front of it; `//`, `///` and `//javascript:alert(1)` are dropped; with no server URL a protocol-relative URL is dropped and a local path is left as it is. Mutation-checked with 5 mutants (the fix reverted, only a triple slash excluded, the local shortcut removed, the shortcut without the server, the condition inverted), each killed by the test meant for it. |
| R742    | `tests/test_notification_system_switch_r742.py`                           | Pattern A + runtime                 | **`system_enabled = false` keeps the MCP server from raising desktop notifications**. The system provider is registered whenever the platform can run it (`plyer` imports, plus `pyobjus` on macOS) and the switch was never looked at, while the MCP server asks for the system channel with every feedback request; so the shipped default (`config.toml.default`: "off by default to avoid unexpected popups") still raised one desktop notification per request, and turning the switch off could not stop them. Locks, with a platform on which the provider works and the real provider factory: with `skip_unavailable=True` a switched-off system channel creates no event and shows nothing; a switched-on one shows exactly one notification; the switch is read at send time (off, on, off in one manager gives 0, 1, 1 notifications); the other channels do not follow it (Bark still goes out while system is off); without the flag the caller's own list is taken as given (the self-test route is unchanged). Mutation-checked with 6 mutants (gate removed, inverted, dropping Bark instead, leaking to callers that did not opt in, reading the sound switch, dropping every channel), each killed by the test meant for it. |
| R743    | `tests/test_css_orphan_selectors_r743.py`                                 | Pattern C + source scan             | **Stylesheets only style classes some code can produce**. Rules outlive the markup they were written for: a keyboard-shortcut hint with its tooltip, a 32 px new-task tab, the `btn-enabled` / `textarea-disabled` state classes, light-theme overrides for the code toolbar's `lang-label` / `copy-btn` while the toolbar renders `language-label` / `copy-button` (the live classes had their own rules, so nothing looked wrong), and the countdown ring and task list of the VS Code panel. Mutation-checked with 10 mutants (an orphan rule re-added to either stylesheet, everything counted as produced, prefixes ignored, partial tokens accepted, an empty or incomplete input set, attribute selectors or at-rules read as classes), each killed by the test meant for it. |
| R744    | `tests/test_copy_button_feedback_timer_r744.py`                           | Pattern C + Node runtime            | **A second click on a code block's copy button keeps its own feedback**. The copy button of the Web UI (`DOMSecurity.createCopyButton`) and the one of the VS Code panel switched to their "copied" or "error" look and scheduled the way back two seconds later without cancelling the timer the previous click had scheduled; two clicks within two seconds let the first timer reset the button while the second click's feedback was still due, and a failure followed by a success carried both state classes until the stale timer ran. | R745    | `tests/test_vscode_host_noise_filter_r745.py`                             | Pattern C + Node runtime            | **The VS Code host tests do not print VS Code's own failed network lookups**. The extension host tests start a real VS Code; while they run its workbench looks up the marketplace entry of the extension under test, chat entitlement data and alerts, and prints an error line (sometimes with a stack) whenever a request fails. Behind a proxy that happens now and then, so a green run carried lines such as `Failed to fetch` or `ERROR:net/socket/ssl_client_socket_impl.cc ... handshake failed` that have nothing to do with the extension. `scripts/run_vscode_host_tests.mjs` runs `vscode-test`, leaves out exactly those lines (and the stack right behind one), says how many it left out, and passes everything else and the exit code through. | R746    | `tests/test_image_preview_alt_text_r746.py`                               | Pattern C + Node runtime            | **The alt text of an image preview is the file name as it is**. `DOMSecurity.createImagePreview` ran the file name through an HTML entity escaper before assigning it to `img.alt`, a DOM property nobody parses as HTML, so `Tom & Jerry's.png` got the alt text `Tom &amp; Jerry&#x27;s.png` and a screen reader read the entities out; the VS Code panel and the image modal already assign the name unchanged. | R747    | `tests/test_unreferenced_js_symbols_r747.py`                              | Pattern C + source scan             | **Every top-level function, class and class member of the page scripts has a user**. Code nothing calls is still read, tested and shipped with every page load, and it misleads: a close-task path no tab could trigger, a submit-button helper whose only call had gone, a tooltip builder for a tooltip that was removed, and, in `validation-utils.js`, a lazy loader, a virtual scroller and two `debounce` copies that only exports and tests kept alive. | R749    | `tests/test_log_level_applied_at_startup_r749.py`                         | Pattern B + subprocess runtime      | **The configured log level is applied when a process starts**. `web_ui.log_level` and `AI_INTERVENTION_AGENT_LOG_LEVEL` were read by `get_log_level_from_config()`, but nothing called `configure_logging_from_config()`: the MCP server stayed at WARNING and the Web UI process logged every HTTP request line, whatever the setting said, so the `DEBUG` the troubleshooting guide asks for gave nothing. The MCP server (`server.main()`, before the `event=server.boot` banner) and the Web UI script (`web_ui.py` `__main__`, before it serves) apply it now, and werkzeug follows the level because it pins its own logger to INFO and otherwise adds a plain stderr handler that skips the log sanitiser. The tests fake the stall, so they hold on every platform: a control shows that stock Werkzeug asks for the name of `127.0.0.1`; the override is installed once and respects a Werkzeug with its own `server_bind`; a server created with `socket.getfqdn` rigged to fail binds, records name and port and answers a request; creating a `WebFeedbackUI` installs it; and the real `web_ui.py`, started the way `ServiceManager` starts it in a process where `socket.getfqdn` sleeps 90 s, still answers `/api/health` within 30 s. | R766    | `tests/test_config_reload_sse_wait_r766.py`                               | Pattern A + runtime                 | **A configuration reload no longer loses the reply to a task that is being waited on**. | R769    | `tests/test_math_spans_protect_tex_r769.py`                               | Pattern A + runtime                 | **Only TeX the page recognised is typeset, and Markdown leaves it alone**. `protectMathDelimiters` hid only `\(...\)` and `\[...\]` from marked, so the TeX inside `$...$` and `$$...$$` was rewritten (`\\` lost a backslash and a matrix collapsed into one row, `\,` became a comma, `\{` and `\}` lost theirs, `_` and `*` turned into emphasis), and MathJax read every `$...$` of the page, so "The total is $5 and the deposit is $10" became one italic formula, and so did option labels, because MathJax also typeset the whole body once after it loaded. The page now scans the text itself with the Pandoc and GitHub rules (the opening `$` is followed by a non-space, the closing one is preceded by a non-space and not followed by a digit, `\$` is an ordinary character; code spans, fences, URLs and image alt texts are skipped), hides what it found from marked, restores the original text inside a `tex2jax_process` span, and MathJax looks only inside those spans (the container carries `tex2jax_ignore`, nothing typesets the whole page). Where TeX cannot be read as TeX it comes back as text: escaped for an attribute inside a tag (a link title, a reference definition, an image alt), plain inside `<pre>` and `<code>`, and without the `>` markers of a blockquote it spans. Eighty-three tests run the real `mathjax-loader.js` and the vendored `marked.js` in Node: 63 inputs with their exact segments and HTML (currency, shell variables, escapes, code spans of every run length, fences, blockquotes, link titles, indented code), seven attacks that must never put an attribute or a script into the HTML, a round trip of more than 25,000 random texts (the restored text equals the source and every segment is well formed), twelve 200 kB inputs made of unmatched openers that must be scanned in under 1.5 s (measured: at most 30 ms), and the MathJax configuration (`typeset` off, the `ignore` and `process` classes, `pre` and `code` skipped). |

Four fail with the previous code; the fifth is the control. Swallowing every refused submit fails the control and R114's own reverse test. |

Real stack: six routes (120 MB multipart, 102 MB JSON) answered 413 HTML / 500 / 500 / 400 / 400 / 400 with 2 tracebacks in the log, now six `413` JSON with none; a 600 KB text part answered `500` / HTML `413`, now `413` JSON; 400 KB unchanged (`200`). |
| R801    | `tests/test_prompt_links_stay_out_of_the_page_r801.py`                    | Pattern A + runtime contract | **A link in the agent's message never takes the approval page away**. marked passes every destination to the DOM unchanged, so a click replaced the page in the same tab (`[docs](https://...)`, a bare URL), landed on the server's own 404 (`[app.py](src/app.py)`: a relative reference can only resolve to the page's origin), reloaded it (`[x]()` is rendered as `<a href="">`), and `javascript:` / `data:` destinations stayed live links that only the CSP stopped, at the price of an error-level console violation per click. `classifyPromptLink` + `processLinks` in `app.js` (called by both `renderMarkdownContent` and `updateDescriptionDisplay`, before math typesetting) now apply the policy the VS Code webview's `sanitizePromptHtml` already had: `http:` / `https:` / `//host` -> `target="_blank"` + `rel="noopener noreferrer"`; `#fragment` / `mailto:` / `tel:` untouched; everything else (relative, empty, `javascript:`, `data:`, `file:`, custom schemes, a scheme that only appears after the browser strips tabs / newlines / spaces) -> the anchor is replaced by its children. 65 tests (49 fail with the policy turned into a no-op; sixteen one-point mutants, all caught): 37 `href` shapes through a hand-written DOM in node (idempotent, mixed content with an image child, `null` / `{}` containers), the vendored marked's real output for 20 link forms through the same policy (and the assertion that marked itself does not filter `javascript:`, `data:`, empty or relative destinations), both call sites ahead of `loadMathJaxIfNeeded`, a tripwire that fails when a third script starts rendering Markdown, and the parity of `target` / `rel` with `sanitizePromptHtml`. `tests/test_prompt_markup_untrusted_r767.py` stubs `processLinks` next to `processCodeBlocks` / `processStrikethrough`. Real Chrome 153, old vs new: the old page navigated its own tab to `example.com`, `example.org` and `/src/app.py` (404) and logged a CSP error on the `javascript:` link; now the tab stays on the Web UI, two tabs open, file and script links are text, `#top` is an in-page jump, `mailto:` is untouched, no console error, on first paint and after a task switch through polling. |

| R804    | `tests/test_docs_match_tool_surface_r804.py`                              | Pattern B + meta-invariant | **The docs describe the tool and the HTTP API the server really has**. `docs/mcp_tools.md` documented a per-call `auto_resubmit_timeout` parameter and a complete Agent-mode example that passes it, and `docs/architecture.md` drew it into two sequence diagrams and the parameter table; `interactive_feedback` has no such parameter and its schema is strict (`additionalProperties: false`), so an agent that copied the example got `Unexpected keyword argument` and no task (real stdio MCP: the Agent-mode example of both languages failed, the six others worked). Real stdio MCP, old docs vs new: the Agent-mode examples fail before and every example of both languages creates a task now (the Agent-mode one with header `Auth`, its placeholder and `[True, False]` defaults); all six architecture diagrams render in mermaid 11 in real Chrome (a deliberately broken one is rejected by the same harness). |

| R809    | `tests/test_webview_submit_keeps_other_task_composer_r809.py`             | Pattern A + runtime contract | **A task's submit in the VS Code webview never touches another task's form**. `packages/vscode/webview-ui.js` shows one feedback form (textarea, pasted images, option check-boxes) and swaps its contents the moment another task's tab is clicked (`switchToTask` → `restoreLocalStateForTask`, before the server has confirmed the activation). Now `isSubmitTargetStillCurrent(taskId)` (no id: current when no task is active; otherwise `activeTaskId === taskId`, the contract of the page) decides whether `submitWithData` empties the live form (the submitted task's saved text, images and option state are still dropped, the other task's are kept), and `autoSubmit` stops the countdown and returns, before the retry throttle, when the countdown's task is not the one on screen. 8 tests run the real function sources (extracted from the file) in Node against stand-ins for the surrounding state: a finished submit leaves the on-screen task's textarea, images, options and cache entries alone and still empties its own task's form, a form without any task id (legacy single-task servers) and a task the poll already picked; a countdown for a task that left the screen sends nothing, stops the countdown and keeps the retry throttle unspent; a countdown for the on-screen task still sends the typed text and ticked options, the resubmit prompt when nothing was typed, and behaves as before when no active task is known. 2 tests fail on the old script (the other 6 pin behaviour that must not change); 15 one-point mutants (guard removed, inverted, ignoring an unknown active task, placed after the retry throttle, countdown not stopped first, helper always true / always false / no-id case never current / comparison inverted, cleanup unconditional, images / options / textarea emptied outside the guard, the submitted task's saved state kept, the other task's saved state dropped) are all caught. Left as it was: when the per-task route answers 404 the webview falls back to the legacy `/api/submit`, which answers whichever task is active. |

Now both get a 2 px ring in the colour of every other ring (`--focus-ring-color`: `rgb(217, 119, 87)` dark, `rgb(179, 90, 60)` light). Two details decide whether it works: the code block's rule is `!important`, and between two `!important` declarations the more specific selector wins, so the ring is `!important` and more specific than the rule that removes it; and `.code-block-container` is `overflow: hidden` with rounded corners, so an outset ring would be cut off and the ring is drawn *inside* the block (`outline-offset: -2px`). | R820    | `tests/test_progress_keepalive_r820.py`                                   | Pattern A + runtime contract | **An unanswered question no longer ends in `MCP error -32001: Request timed out` after two minutes**. `interactive_feedback` waits for a person and the server sent nothing while it waited. The Cursor IDE's MCP client gives every tool call an idle timeout (120 s by default, kept between 5 s and 30 min) that it restarts each time a `notifications/progress` for the call arrives, up to one hour in total; it sends `_meta.progressToken` with every `tools/call` and sets `resetTimeoutOnProgress`. Cursor's command-line agent and `agent acp` are different: their timeout is a fixed 60 s that progress does not restart, so this keepalive cannot help there (troubleshooting section 20). A person who needed longer than two minutes, or a panel that was not on screen and so never counted down to its automatic submit, got the error, the task the call had made was cleaned up behind it (R775) and the answer that came later went to a request nobody waited for (three questions in a row failed after 120 s in a real Cursor session). The wait now runs inside `_client_keepalive(ctx)`: every 20 s (`_PROGRESS_KEEPALIVE_INTERVAL_S`) `ctx.report_progress` sends `progress` = seconds waited and the message `Waiting for human feedback (N s)`, with no `total` because the wait can end early or be extended, and the spec wants `progress` to grow strictly. The first beat comes one interval after the wait starts; the block cancels and awaits its beat on every way out (return, error, anyio cancel scope, plain task cancel), so no notification follows the result and no task outlives the call, even when the send itself is stuck on a client that stopped reading. A failing send is logged at debug level and retried at the next beat; without a `ctx` (tests, direct calls) nothing is scheduled; a client that sent no progress token gets nothing (`report_progress` is then a no-op) and one that does not restart on progress is unaffected. Trade-off: a call now stays open for as long as the backend wait (countdown + 40 s, at least 260 s; `backend_max_wait` caps only the first wait, and a countdown that still has time left extends it) instead of ending with an error at the client's idle timeout. 18 tests, 14 red on the old code, six of them through a real FastMCP server and `Client`, in both protocol modes; every scenario runs on a deadline thread, so a regression fails instead of hanging the suite. 17 of 17 one-point mutants caught (no wrapper, wrapper after the wait, interval 200 s / 25 s / 0.5 s, beat never cancelled, cancelled beat not awaited, progress constant, progress not in seconds, a total, no message, clock at the epoch, send error not swallowed, `BaseException` swallowed, a beat at once, a beat without a `ctx`, send before sleep). Real MCP SDK 1.32.1 client with Cursor's call options (120 s idle timeout, restart on progress, 1 h total) against the real stdio server and Web UI, old tree against new: answer sent 150 s after the call, old failed with `-32001` at 120.5 s (0 notifications), new returned the answer at 150.5 s (7 notifications); no answer at all with a 20 s countdown, new returned the resubmit prompt at 264 s, the backend's own 260 s limit (13 notifications), instead of failing at 120 s. |
| R821    | `tests/test_sse_reconnect_backoff_r821.py`                                | Pattern A + runtime contract | **A stream that the server accepts and then drops is not reconnected once a second for ever**. `EventSource.onopen` of the Web UI page (`multi_task.js`) and of the VS Code panel's webview (`webview-ui.js`) set `_sseReconnectDelay` back to its 1 s base, so the back-off (1, 2, 4 ... 30 s) only ever grew for a server that could not be reached at all. A server, a proxy or a captive portal that answers `/api/events` with `200` and then ends the stream (a reverse proxy that buffers or times out the response, a process that crashes right after accepting) started every cycle at the base again: one connection a second for as long as the page was open, each of them also flipping the status dot and the task polling between their 30 s safety net and their 2 s pace (with a task fetch on every drop). R796 made it worse in one respect: the stream now opens with a comment frame, so `onopen` fires at once instead of after the first event or heartbeat, and nothing separates "accepted" from "working" any more. The VS Code host (`extension.ts`, R718) already had the right rule, `SSE_STABLE_CONNECTION_MS = 10000`: the delay goes back to the base only when the connection that ended had lived that long. Now `onopen` records `_sseOpenedAtMs` and leaves the delay alone, and `onerror` calls `_settleSseBackoff()` before it reads the delay or schedules anything: a stream that lived 10 s or more restarts the back-off at 1 s, a shorter one keeps doubling up to the 30 s cap. Real Chrome 153 against a proxy that answers `/api/events` with `: connected` and ends the stream 300 ms later, old tree against new: 23 connections in 30 s, 1.3 s apart, now 5, at 0.6, 2.0, 4.3, 8.6 and 16.9 s (gaps 1.3, 2.3, 4.3 and 8.3 s); a healthy stream that lives 12 s and then drops reconnects after 1 s on both trees (4 connections in 40 s, 13 s apart). Please access over HTTPS or localhost/127.0.0.1 and retry. — <translated hint>` on every new request, in a Chinese page too, and nothing in it said that a request had arrived; every other reason (permission denied, unsupported, ...) passes the real title and message and lets the translated hint say why. | R823    | `tests/test_bark_url_whitespace_r823.py`                                  | Pattern A + runtime contract | **A Bark address pasted with a blank after it, or written in capitals, works**. | R824    | `tests/test_terminal_notifier_value_escape_r824.py`                       | Pattern A + runtime contract | **A request that starts with a bullet, a bracket, a parenthesis or a quote reaches the macOS desktop at once, with its words as they were**. The binary hands `-title` and `-message` to the argument parser of `NSUserDefaults`, which reads a value that starts with `[ ( { < " -` as a property list or an option: the binary then has "no message" and waits for one on its standard input (`execFile` gives it a pipe that never closes), the provider's 2.5 s timeout kills it, and the AppleScript fallback raises the banner late, by another path and with another sound; a value that is nothing but a property list (`()`, `{}`, `(x)`) hangs it whatever its stdin is; a value that starts with a backslash loses it; a title read as "no title" becomes `Terminal`. Measured on the bundled binary (every printable ASCII character as the first character of a value, four shapes each, 404 texts, title and message alike): 21 read wrong (`[ ( { < " -` and `\`, three shapes each), 380 right, 3 that start with a no-break space (which the provider trims anyway). Real provider with real `execFile` and the real binary, the banner read back with `-list`, 13 summaries: old tree 2 of 13 right (10 waited 2 504 to 2 510 ms and fell back to AppleScript, 1 lost its first backslash), new tree 13 of 13 at once (89 to 214 ms). Now `escapeTerminalNotifierValue` puts one backslash in front of the `-title` and `-message` values: the parser sees a backslash first and stops treating the value as a property list, and the binary removes exactly that backslash (401 of 404 right; the 3 left are the no-break-space ones). `-sound`, `-activate` and `-execute` are not touched, and the AppleScript fallback receives the text as it was. The module reads the source (every `-title` / `-message` value goes through the helper, the helper is exported, no raw value is left, the other arguments are untouched) and transpiles the TypeScript to run it in Node with a fake `vscode`, a fake `child_process.execFile` and `process.platform` set to `darwin`, so the arguments the provider really builds are read on any machine (skipped when `node` or the `typescript` package is missing). `packages/vscode/test/notification-providers.test.js` repeats it on a real macOS host (5 tests) and, when asked (`AIIA_REAL_TERMINAL_NOTIFIER=1`), runs the real binary the way the provider does: every escaped text is shown as it is and at once, and the unescaped ones are still misread, so the day a newer binary reads them right the escape can go. 86 tests here (80 red on the old provider, run against `git show HEAD:` of the file) and 21 one-point mutants (the helper returning the value, doubling the backslash or putting it at the end, escaping only `[`, or all but one of `-` / `\` / `(` / `"`, doubling the backslashes inside the value, the title or the message left raw, the two swapped, `-sound`, `-activate` or `-execute` escaped, `-sound` changed, `-ignoreDnD` always sent, the fallback given the escaped title or message, the helper not exported) all caught. |
| R825    | `tests/test_web_ui_revived_during_wait_r825.py`                           | Pattern A + runtime contract | **A Web UI that dies while a question is waiting is started again, instead of leaving the call hanging until the backend timeout**. Now the poll counts consecutive `httpx.ConnectError`s (nothing listens on the port; a timeout, a read error or any HTTP answer, a 5xx included, means a process is there, and resets the count). After `_WEB_UI_LOST_PROBES` (3) of them it awaits `service_manager.ensure_web_ui_running(config)`, which health-checks and starts the Web UI (another window may have started it first), and goes on waiting: a restored task is answered as before, a task the new Web UI does not know is a 404 and returns the resubmit prompt at once. At most `_WEB_UI_REVIVE_MAX` (3) attempts per outage, three refused polls apart; a failed attempt (the port taken by another program: `port_in_use`) is logged and the wait carries on to its timeout; any HTTP answer from the Web UI starts the count again; a cancel is never swallowed. Same two windows, old tree against new: old, the Web UI stayed down for the 80 s the script looked and the call stayed pending; new, a warning names the attempt 8 to 9 s after the window closed (three polls 2 s apart, and each refused request also waits through the 1.5 s back-off of the default `http_max_retries=3`), the Web UI is back 11.1 s after the window closed with the second window's task restored, and the answer submitted there reaches the call 1.2 s later. The policy lives in a small class, `_WebUILossWatch` (`refusal()`, `answered()`, `revive_due()`), and the restart in a module-level `_revive_web_ui`, so `wait_for_task_completion` (already past McCabe 20) gains two branches and not six; the policy is asked directly, without a server: the two documented numbers, a fresh watch, two refusals are not enough, attempts at refusals 3, 6 and 9 and then the cap, an ask that gets a no is not counted, an answer starts the refusals over and gives the next outage a full budget; and the helper: it restarts with the wait's own config and its log line names the task, the count and the attempt, a failed restart is logged and not raised, a cancel goes through. 23 tests (13 run the wait against the server or the scripted client, 9 of them red on the old code and the 4 that stay green are the 'do not restart' ones, held by the mutants; 7 ask the policy; 3 run the helper) and 19 one-point mutants (threshold 1 or 4, a cap of 2 or 4, no reset on an answer at the call site, `answered()` keeping the count or the used attempts, counting double or not at all, no cap, attempts not spaced, an ask that is not recorded, every exception counted, the poll loop never asking, a cancel swallowed, the restart a no-op or given the wrong config, a failed restart propagating, the log hiding the attempt number) all caught. |
| R826    | `tests/test_log_traceback_hides_variable_values_r826.py`                  | Pattern A + runtime contract | **A log traceback does not print the values of the variables it passes through**. `enhanced_logging` added its stderr sink without `diagnose`, and loguru's default is `True`: under every code line of a traceback it prints the values of the variables that line uses. `LogSanitizer` rewrites only `record["message"]`, so none of that went through it, and the convention of logging lengths and never content (`task.created … message_len=22`) stopped at the traceback. A Bark push to a refused port: 114 lines with the notification text once, now 50 and none. The traceback itself (frames, line numbers, code lines) is still logged. | R827    | `tests/test_files_with_secrets_are_owner_only_r827.py`                    | Pattern A + runtime contract | **A file the agent creates that can hold a secret is readable by its owner only**. `config.toml` holds `notification.bark_device_key` and `network_security.api_token`; the first run copied the template with `shutil.copyfile`, which creates the file as `0666 & ~umask` (`0644` under the usual `umask 022`, `0666` under `umask 0`: containers, `UMask=0000`). New files are created `0600` whatever the `umask` (`config_utils.write_text_owner_only`, `restrict_to_owner`); a file that already exists keeps its mode, except the backup and the in-flight `.tmp`, which the agent generates itself and tightens whenever it rewrites them. | R828    | `tests/test_json_nesting_cap_r828.py`                                     | Pattern A + runtime contract | **A JSON body nested far deeper than any client sends is refused on every Python version, and a text setting never stores the `repr` of a list or an object**. The sweep found a bug that does not depend on the interpreter: the text settings of `/api/update-notification-config` ran every value through `str()`, so `{"barkDeviceKey": ["abc"]}` was stored as `['abc']` and answered "updated" although the route's contract is `400 invalid_value` with nothing applied; a list or an object is now refused with the field named (a number is still stored as its text, `null` still clears). Old tree against new, same bodies: `500` or `200` after 0.6–1.1 s → `400` after 5–16 ms, and the health probe never waited more than 15 ms; every write route x 147 bodies (a deep array, a deep object and a deep value under each key a handler reads) at 100 000 levels: 13 answers `5xx` on 3.14 before, none on 3.11, 3.12, 3.13 and 3.14 now, with the same 1 911 refusals and 882 other answers on each. 86 tests (63 red on the old code on 3.11, 70 on 3.14; the others are the contract that must not change: the interpreter that already refuses 60 000 levels, flat text settings, numbers, `null`, the enum, a readable array, the task-scoped submit) and 23 one-point mutants (the cap and its two off-by-ones, each container type, the first-child-only shortcuts at the root and below, a limit of zero, the loop going on over the parent after a push, the stack of tuples whose memory follows the width, the `RecursionError` conversion, the provider and its install, the optional-body fallback, the form check, the structured-value check and its shapes) all caught. |

| R866    | `tests/test_deployment_guide_proxy_snippets_r866.py`                      | Pattern A + runtime contract  | **The proxy settings the deployment guides show give the Web UI the visitor's address**. Behind a reverse proxy on the same machine every connection reaches the Web UI from 127.0.0.1, so the access control, the rate limiter and the routes only this machine may call tell visitors apart by the `X-Forwarded-For` header the proxy writes. The guides' nginx block for the event stream set `Connection` and nothing else, and nginx does not carry the `proxy_set_header` lines of the block around a `location` into one that sets its own, so the stream reached the Web UI without a client address: with the guides' snippets in front of Waitress, Waitress with its trust flags, gunicorn and Flask's server (nginx 1.31 in containers), a visitor on `blocked_ips` was answered 403 on every route but the stream, which answered 200 and `: connected`. And Waitress, run as the guides say, deletes `X-Forwarded-For` (and the other forwarded headers) unless it is told which proxy to trust (its default since 3.0), so behind nginx every visitor was this machine: another machine got 200 from `api-token-info` and a visitor on `blocked_ips` got 200 on every route. The guides now write the header in the stream's block, give the Waitress command that trusts the proxy (`--trusted-proxy=127.0.0.1 --trusted-proxy-headers=x-forwarded-for --trusted-proxy-count=1`) and add two lines to the validation checklist that ask from another machine (`api-token-info` must answer 403, and the stream must refuse a visitor on `blocked_ips`). With those, all four servers answered that visitor 403 on every route, the stream included; an allowed visitor got `: connected` within a hundredth of a second, you browsing from the server through nginx were still this machine, and a CDN in front of nginx (two hops) was judged by the address the CDN wrote. This module (32 tests, 7 of which fail on the previous guides; 46 of 46 one-point mutants killed) locks: the nginx snippets of both guides, read as nginx reads them (comments aside): the stream and the rest are the two locations that reach the Web UI, each writes `X-Forwarded-For` once, as an address nginx saw and never as the visitor's own header, and the two guides show the same configuration, with the rule's own cases; the Waitress command that names a trusted proxy (the loopback address, the header, as many proxies as `trusted_proxy_hops` has by default; the same in both guides); the checklist lines that ask `api-token-info` and the stream through the proxy; and, with the real routes behind the real hooks, a table of visitors (what nginx writes for a blocked one, an allowed one and you; a missing header; a CDN at two hops and at one; 127.0.0.1 written in front of the chain; no proxy) against `api-token-info`, `/api/health` and the stream, and every `GET` route refusing the visitor the proxy named, the stream included and without subscribing to anything. |

| R876    | `tests/test_reload_drops_the_scheduled_save_r876.py`                      | Pattern A + runtime contract  | **A reload drops the save that was scheduled before it, whoever scheduled it**. `ConfigManager` writes behind a 3 s debounce (`set`, `update`, `import_config` and `update_section` start a `threading.Timer`), and `reload()` (what the file watcher calls) and `restore_config()` replace the in-memory config with the file on disk, so whatever was waiting to be saved has to go: the external edit wins. `_load_config` cancelled the timer only `if self._pending_changes`, and `update_section` (the settings page) records no pending key, so its timer outlived the reload and, up to three seconds later, serialised the config it had just loaded into the file: a hand-written file that lacks keys the defaults have (55 bytes in the probe, edited by hand within the debounce window of a settings change) came back with every default key added (1,048 bytes). | R879    | `tests/test_release_workflow_immutable_attest_r879.py`                    | Pattern A + fake `gh`         | **The release job survives release immutability, and signs what it ships**. Release immutability is a repository setting (generally available since October 2025): once a Release is published, its tag and its assets are locked, and GitHub adds a signed release attestation. The `github-release` job of `release.yml` predates it and had two problems that the setting turns from harmless into breaking. Its step made the Release with `gh release create`, or, when the tag already had one, uploaded with `gh release upload --clobber`; that step is what a re-run reaches when the clean-up of old Releases fails after the publish, and on an immutable Release the upload is refused (the job turns red) while on a mutable one it swaps the files under a Release that has been public for minutes. And `gh release view` finds a draft by its tag, so a draft left behind by a killed runner sent the step down the same upload branch, which ends green and leaves the draft a draft (from the source of `gh`; not tried on GitHub). The job now looks at the tag's Release first (a leftover draft stops it with an error that says what to delete, a published Release skips everything up to the clean-up), has `actions/attest` write build provenance for the sdist, the wheel and the VSIX, keeps the bundle as `ai-intervention-agent-<version>.sigstore.json` (the suffix OpenSSF Scorecard's Signed-Releases looks for) and verifies every file against it, with `--repo` and `--signer-workflow`, before anything goes out; then it creates the Release as a draft, uploads the four files, compares names and sha256 digests with the local ones, publishes last, and reports whether the Release is immutable and has its attestation in notices only. Locks, static: the job's permissions are exactly `actions: read`, `contents: write`, `id-token: write` and `attestations: write`, and no other job (not `build`, which runs `npm ci` and the CI gate) gets an OIDC token or write access to attestations; `actions/attest` is pinned to a commit with its `# vX.Y.Z` comment; the attested files are the shipped files; step order and gates; the draft flags and the publish after the check; no `--clobber`, no `--cleanup-tag` and no `::warning::` anywhere. Locks, run: each scripted step runs under `bash -e` against a fake `gh` that models drafts, published and immutable Releases, asset digests, a paginated Release list and attestation verification, and refuses what GitHub refuses. A new Release (draft, upload, check, publish in that order, exact digests), a leftover draft (stops, nothing changed), a re-run after the publish with immutability on and off (nothing changed, exit 0), a corrupt upload (found while still a draft), a bundle that does not verify (no Release exists yet), a prerelease tag, a slow or missing release attestation and a failed read after the publish (exit 0 in all three). The previous step is kept verbatim in the module and shown to fail on an immutable Release, to swap files on a mutable one and to end green with a draft unpublished. `actions/attest` and the real API are not run; the flags of `gh attestation verify` and `gh release verify` were tried by hand on a real bundle and a real immutable Release of `cli/cli`. 24 tests, 19 of which fail on the previous workflow; 36 of 36 mutants killed, 28 of them by the scripted runs alone. |
