This module (70 tests, 48 of which fail on the previous tree, the other 22 are controls; 48 one-point mutants, all killed) locks: the real `webview-settings-ui.js` run in Node against a small DOM that models focus (a browser does not focus what is inert, disabled or not rendered), `inert` and document-level key events - open and close (inert set and removed for exactly the parts the code marked, an earlier `inert` kept, the first opener kept on a second open, the fallback to the rendered settings button, a closed dialog not taking focus), Escape and the cases it must leave alone, the Tab wrap at both ends (disabled, `hidden` and collapsed controls at the ends of the list), and a table of typed countdown values with what the field then shows and what is saved; the markup the behaviour depends on (`tabindex`, the two settings buttons, the toast host and the dialog as `body` children); the toast host above the dialog and the container without a focus ring; the message in all three languages; and the countdown limits equal to the server's `AUTO_RESUBMIT_TIMEOUT_MIN` / `MAX`. `tests/test_frontend_input_range_parity.py` now pins the extension's `FEEDBACK_COUNTDOWN_MAX` constant instead of the old `v <= 3600` check. |
| R901    | `tests/test_options_unpin_r901.py`                                        | Pattern A + Node runtime + CSS contract | **The options of a request can be unpinned, so they go back to the end of the message**. TODO "界面优化 1": the multi-select options are pinned above the input box by default (R818 for the VS Code panel, R819 for the Web UI), and the maintainer also wanted them to be unpinnable, "so that, like before that feature, they hang off the end of the content". Both surfaces now carry a pin toggle: a button with `aria-pressed` whose label ("Pin options") is the same in both states, so a screen reader reads "Pin options, toggle button, pressed" and never a label that contradicts the state. The Web UI button (`#options-pin-btn`) sits in a 28 px strip at the top of the tray (the top padding of the list went from 1.25rem to 1.75rem, so the button covers neither the first option nor the 8 px scroll bar); the panel button (`#optionsPinBtn`) shares the head row with the label (22 px, `margin: -5px 0` so that its margin box is no taller than a line of the label, and painted above the grip it overlaps by a pixel or two), so the pinned dock keeps its size to the pixel. Unpinned is the layout from before the dock existed. In the Web UI `.options-dock.is-unpinned` takes the tray out of the sticky layout (`position: relative; bottom: auto; z-index: auto`): the phone layout lowers the sticky edge by 1.25rem inside a media query, so every unpinned rule has to outweigh every rule it undoes (`position`, `bottom`, `z-index`, and on the list `max-height`, `overflow`, `padding`, the frame and the shadow), which the test reads out of the stylesheet instead of comparing two literals; the list loses its frame, its cap, its scroll and its bottom padding, the grip is hidden, and nothing is kept clear at the end of the page (`--aiia-options-reserve: 0px`). In the panel `initOptionsPin()` moves the dock out of the form (between the message scroller and the input box) to the end of the scroller and back to exactly the same place (`.options-dock.is-unpinned`: no cap, no margins, no clipping, no grip), and the focus stays on the button across the move (a moved node loses it: `focus({ preventScroll: true })`, with a fallback for engines that reject the options). The choice is remembered: the Web UI keeps `aiia.feedbackOptionsPinned.v1` in `localStorage` (only "unpinned" is stored, so no key, a broken value or another schema means pinned, and a storage that throws leaves the toggle working for the page); the panel keeps `optionsPinned` in the saved webview state with the rest. Toggling never touches the height set with the grip or the scroll position, and a task without options shows neither the button nor an empty strip. Measured in a real Chrome 156 on a 14-finding message with 12 options: pinned, the tray is the same box as before (360 px at 1280 x 900, 358 px at 390 x 844) and the page the same height, with the list 8 px lower inside it; unpinned, the page is the old page plus the 28 px strip (3 430 px -> 3 458 px at 1280 x 900), and on a 390 px phone 28 px more above the list and 8 px less under it (3 736 px -> 3 756 px: the list used to be a flex item of the form, whose margin did not collapse into the next block, and is now inside the tray). Driven by real mouse and key events in a real VS Code 1.110.0: the pinned dock is the same box as in the previous extension (list at y 399, 265 px tall), a click and Enter and Space toggle it with the focus (and its ring) staying on the button, Tab goes from the grip to the button to the first option, the choice survives the sidebar being hidden and shown again, a short task shows its three options right under its message, and a task without options shows nothing, in the dark and the light theme, with no console error or warning. This module (90 tests, 80 of which fail on the old files; the other 10 are controls: the default state, a page that lacks the button or the tray, the label that does not change, rules that must stay as they are) pins: the template and the stylesheet of both surfaces (the button's attributes and place, its room, hit area and colours in both states, the contrast of those colours in each theme, every rule the unpinned layout undoes and the weight of its selectors, the `:has()` rule that protects the message not matching a dock inside the scroller, `.hidden` still winning); the real `feedback_options_dock.js` in Node against a small DOM (the reserve in both states, the stored value read and written in every shape, a click that reserves and un-reserves at once, `init()` twice leaving one listener, `destroy()`, a page without the button or the tray, storage that throws); the real `initOptionsPin`, `restorePersistedUiState` and `schedulePersistUiState` of the panel in Node against a DOM that models moving a node and losing focus (the dock leaves and returns to exactly its place, the focus after every click, the saved state, the restore independent of the height); and the strings in the four locale files of each surface. 102 one-point mutants are all caught. `tests/test_web_ui_options_dock_r819.py` lists the three names the script now exports. |
| R902    | `tests/test_no_task_animations_pause_r902.py`                             | Pattern A + Node runtime + CSS contract | **What moves on the no-task page stands still while nobody is looking at it**. TODO "无任务动画在失焦/不可见时暂停": the Web page paused its Lottie sprout when the tab was hidden and nothing else (losing the focus paused nothing, the CSS loops never stopped), and the VS Code panel stopped nothing. Now, while the page is hidden or its window is not in front, the class `aiia-ambient-paused` on `<html>` freezes every CSS animation of the no-task state (`animation-play-state: paused`, `::before` and `::after` included: `#no-content-container` in the Web page, `#noContentState` in the panel) and the sprout is `pause()`d, then `resize()`d and `play()`ed from the frame where it stopped (no new animation, no new fetch, no seek). The Web page learns "in front" from events only (`blur` / `focus` of the window, `visibilitychange`, a persisted `pageshow`, and `pointerdown` / `keydown` in the capture phase as a safety net) and never asks `document.hasFocus()`, which stays `false` for good in an embedded view; the default is "in front", so a page nobody told anything keeps moving. The panel cannot see its window (the `blur` / `focus` of a webview follow the focus inside its frame), so the extension host posts `{type: "window-focus", focused}` from `WebviewProvider` on every window state change, after the queue held until `ready` is flushed, and whenever the view is shown again; the panel applies it, follows its own `visibilitychange` and `pointerdown` / `keydown`, and ignores its own `blur` / `focus`. Measured in real programs with real focus changes (Finder in front, then the program again; medians of three, % of one core): Chrome 156 with a window on a 120 Hz display, the old page 16.2 % in front and 17.1 % with another app in front, now 16.9 % and 1.8 % (renderer 1.0, GPU 0.02, no style recalculation instead of 120 a second); headless Chrome at 60 Hz, 10.2 % before, 10.7 % in front, 1.0 % behind, 11.6 % in front again; VS Code 1.110.0, the no-task panel, 14.9 % in the webview process (35.6 % counting every process) in front and 0.18 % (0.9 %) with the window behind; the sprout goes on at the frame where it stopped (37.2 -> 38.8, 160 ms after the window is back); a panel shown or reloaded while the window is behind comes up frozen; no console warning or error. Two things the checks taught: a plain Chrome delivers `blur` / `focus` about 80-100 ms after the app switch, but a click synthesised through the debugging protocol gives the page the focus without the window, so no `blur` follows, which is why the real-Chrome check opens the page from the command line; and the no-task page has a button that shuts the service down (`#close-btn`), so a test that clicks the middle of it takes its own server away. Limits, on purpose: a Web page loaded while its window is behind, or whose window never owned the focus (the address bar had it), is told nothing and moves until the first focus, blur or use; the panel follows the window, not itself, so a visible panel in a window in front moves while the editor has the focus; the breathing status light of the panel's no-task state freezes with the rest, the one in the header is not part of it. This module (55 tests, 46 of which fail on the old files; the other 9 are controls: a page nobody told anything keeps moving, a page restored from the cache is in front, the document is never asked, a retired animation stays retired, a round trip without warning, the freeze rule only freezes and no id selector beats it, in both stylesheets) pins: the real `app.js` and the `app.min.js` the page loads in Node against the harness of R727 (blur, focus, hidden, shown, repeated and out-of-order events, a sprout created or rebuilt while out of front, the listeners installed once, a class list that throws), the real block of `webview-ui.js` in Node against a small DOM and how it is wired, both stylesheets (everything that moves can be frozen, the rule only freezes, no id selector beats it), and the host (`webview.ts`: the message and its three call sites). `packages/vscode/test/webview-window-focus.test.js` runs the real `WebviewProvider` in a real VS Code (4 tests). 86 one-point mutants of the five sources are all caught, and 7 of 7 host mutants by the mocha tests. |

| R905    | `tests/test_shutdown_closes_waiting_tasks_r905.py`                       | Pattern B + subprocess runtime | **A question that is still waiting for its answer does not outlive the process that asked it**. TODO "仔细分析和深度思考以及网络搜索……确定代码没有逻辑上的错误、一致性错误、低级错误": when a window is closed while `interactive_feedback` waits, the clean-up of the abandoned wait lives in the event loop, which ends before its first request has come back (`asyncio.run` cancels it), and a SIGTERM ends the server with no clean-up at all (the SIGINT / SIGTERM handlers of `ServiceManager` are never installed in a real server: its first instance is built in the worker thread of `asyncio.to_thread`, where `signal.signal` raises ValueError); the task stays in the Web UI, or in `tasks-<port>.json` for the next Web UI to restore, as the first and active question that nobody is waiting for. Now every task the call creates is listed (`_INFLIGHT_TASKS`; it leaves the list when the answer was handed over, when a close was confirmed with 200 / 404, when the Web UI refused it with a 4xx or when the request never left; a 5xx or an unanswered request keeps it there) and the first listing installs three exit paths for `_close_inflight_tasks_sync` (one synchronous request per task, its own client, 1.5 s for all): a hook that `ServiceManager.cleanup_all` runs before it terminates anything, an atexit callback, and a SIGTERM handler that closes the tasks and lets the process die of the signal as before (installed only while SIGTERM is at its default). It does not build a `ServiceManager`: built on the main thread it installs handlers whose `KeyboardInterrupt` leaves the interpreter waiting for the thread that reads stdin, and a real server did not exit after SIGTERM. This module (52 tests, 50 of which fail on the old code; the 2 controls are the Ctrl-C ones) runs a real child interpreter against a loopback Web UI, with and without a `ServiceManager` built in a worker thread (the runner cancels the call and the loop ends at once, Ctrl-C, SIGTERM with a thread blocked on the open stdin pipe: the process must die of the signal at once, with the task closed), pins the order in `cleanup_all`, the close requests (against a real server, one that never answers, with a clock the test moves, a proxy in the environment, an id with `/?#`) and the list through every way a call can end; 44 one-point mutants are all caught. |

| R911    | `tests/test_task_queue_random_operations_r911.py`                        | Pattern A + runtime contract | **The task queue keeps its promises whatever order the operations come in (seeded random operations against a model, a restart after every step, several threads)**. Nothing checked that, so a rule that holds on every path the author thought of could fail on the one nobody did. This module found nothing wrong (the queue behaves as specified) and pins it. 300 seeded runs of 70 operations (add, switch, answer, remove, the three cleanups, clear all, a read) from a pool of 8 ids against a capacity of 5 are applied to the real queue and to a model of a few lines, and compared after every step (return value, every task's status, the order, the active task, the stored answer, the status events); the read-only invariants (at most one active, one whenever a task is unanswered, the counts, `get_first_incomplete_task`, `has_tasks`) are read from the queue alone; a reach test fails when the runs stop reaching the cases that matter (a full queue, a taken id, a second answer, a hand-over by an answer or by a removal, an answered task being activated, the last unanswered task leaving); seven scenarios state the facts the model must agree with (hand-over in queue order, the first answer for good, the events of a switch, an answer and a removal). On disk (40 runs of 45 steps, with extend, freeze and the hot update of the timeout, R835): after every step a queue loaded from the file equals the live one without its answered tasks (fields, order, active task, loop ledger); clearing everything forgets the ledger and deletes the file. With threads: four writers and a reader on a slow disk with short time slices (every atomic view keeps the promises, and the file ends up equal to the queue), and 25 rounds of four removals released together on a disk whose writes take a random time (the snapshot belongs inside the critical section that writes it, R834). 352 tests, all green on the current code (also on Python 3.13); 46 one-point mutants of `task_queue.py` are all caught (the R834 one in 5 of 5 runs). |
| R912    | `tests/test_task_submit_needs_an_answer_r912.py`                         | Pattern A + runtime contract | **The submit routes do not close a question with a request that holds no answer**. R829 had found it on `/api/submit`: a body that is not a form was read as "no answer", ended the question the user was waiting on with an empty one and answered `200`. The route the page and the VS Code panel really use, `POST /api/tasks/<id>/submit`, was never given the same look: it reads `request.form.get("feedback_text", "")` and `request.form.get("selected_options", "[]")`, and a request that is not a form gives it both defaults. So no body, a JSON document that *holds* the real answer (text and ticks thrown away, the sender told "submitted"), `null`, an array, JSON that does not parse, plain text, bytes, a multipart body Werkzeug cannot read (no boundary, a boundary that does not match, cut off, zero bytes), an answer that sits in the query string, and a form whose fields are not the two an answer is made of (`user_input=...`) all completed the task with `{"user_input": "", "selected_options": []}` and answered `200`; the second answer, the real one, is then a `409` (R851). The form branch of `/api/submit` had the same hole one level down: a form with no answer field in it (`user_input=...`, junk pairs, other fields only) completed the task it named, or the active one, with the same empty answer. On the real stdio MCP server with its Web UI, each of six such bodies on the task route and four such forms on `/api/submit` made the agent's `interactive_feedback` call return "用户未提供任何内容" (the user supplied nothing). Now a request that has neither `feedback_text` nor `selected_options` (empty or not, with or without other fields beside them) and no uploaded file is `400 {"success": false, "status": "error", "error": "invalid_body", "message": ...}` on both routes (`request_holds_an_answer` and `no_answer_response` in `_task_answer.py` hold the rule; `feedback.bodyMustBeForm`, en / zh-CN / zh-TW); the question stays open and untouched, nobody is told, and the next proper form is taken. What R829 decided stays: a JSON object on `/api/submit`, an empty one included, is an answer for the active task (the R348 baseline posts `{}`), and its unreadable JSON bodies keep their own envelope. The page, the VS Code panel and the auto-resubmit always send both fields, also when nothing was typed or ticked, so what they send is unchanged: on the same real server the bodies are `400` and the agent keeps waiting until a form arrives, and a real Chrome 156 typing into the real page gets `200` and the agent receives the typed text. Where the checks sit: the task is looked up first (`404` for a task that is not there, whatever the body), then the body (`400`), then `selected_options` (the old `400`s), then the queue (`409`). 149 tests (105 fail on the old code; the 44 that pass pin what did not change): 18 bodies x the envelope, the question left open with no status event, the next answer taken, the `404` first, the body judged before the queue is asked; six forms that still answer, each as urlencoded and as multipart (text only, options only, both empty as the page sends them, one tick without text, an empty text alone, other fields beside the answer), a body that is only a picture, a picture next to the text; the old `selected_options` `400` and the `409` unchanged; on `/api/submit`: 17 of the bodies refused with nothing completed in the shared queue, the same envelope as the task route for the three forms that are new, a form that names the task not answering it, the forms and the picture that hold an answer still answering, a JSON object still an answer there and only there; the refusal logged once at warning level with the content type (cut at 80 characters) and the length and no text of the request, the task named on the task route only; the message in all three languages (it names both fields and both encodings) and through the route with `Accept-Language`; and the OpenAPI texts (the task route's `400`: the code and the four properties, still `multipart/form-data` only; the `/api/submit` `400`: both fields, `success`, the form case in the error text, JSON still declared). 44 one-point mutants (every way the predicate can be wrong: everything or nothing an answer, files or fields ignored, one field name dropped, misspelt or added, `and` for `or`, `all` for `any`, a field that must be non-empty, the query string counted; every part of the envelope: status 422 / 200, another code, no `success`, no `status`, a message outside the catalogue or another one; the refusal removing the question, its log line leaking the form, at the wrong level, naming no task, with the content type not cut; each call site negated, removed, placed after the answer was taken or before the lookup, the 404 gone, the legacy check moved to its JSON branch; a language missing, the docs weakened) are all caught. |
| R705    | `tests/test_options_render_single_source_r705.py`                       | Pattern C + JS contract     | **Predefined-options single render source guard (body-only page root cause)**. Locks `loadConfig` delegating options rendering to `updateOptionsDisplay` (multi_task.js loads before app.js — template order also locked); the guarded local fallback must clear the container and toggle `hidden`/`visible` classes, never inline `style.display` (the container's initial `.hidden` is `display:none !important`, so inline block left options in the DOM but invisible). Also locks the `lastLoadedDetailsTaskId` watermark: polling retries `loadTaskDetails` every cycle until the active task's details have rendered successfully once — the old condition never retried in the pending-task scenario, leaving the page body-only forever after a single failed fetch.                |
| R706    | `tests/test_bark_custom_scheme_url_r706.py`                             | Pattern A + provider contract | **Bark custom-scheme tap-through guard (shortcuts:// deep links)**. Locks `_is_acceptable_bark_click_url` accepting any RFC 3986 `scheme://` URL (so `bark_url_template = "shortcuts://run-shortcut?name=..."` reaches the phone) while `javascript:` / `data:` (no authority) stay rejected; loopback suppression only applies to http(s) (`shortcuts://localhost` must not be misclassified, `http://localhost:8080` keeps being suppressed); metadata URL candidates are validated the same way with garbage values falling back to the template.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| R707    | `tests/test_ios_a2hs_server_dismiss_r707.py`                            | Pattern A + config/endpoint | **iOS A2HS banner server-side dismiss guard (Shortcuts WebView localStorage loss)**. Locks the `web_ui.ios_a2hs_hint_dismissed` config field (default false), the idempotent any-origin `POST /api/system/ios-a2hs-dismiss` endpoint (banner only shows on remote iOS devices; already-true skips the config write), the `window.AIIA_IOS_A2HS_DISMISSED` template injection, and the frontend contract that the injected flag is checked before localStorage and the dismiss handler fire-and-forget POSTs back — SFSafariViewController neither shares nor persists localStorage, so a client-only dismiss reappeared on every Shortcuts visit.                                                                                                                                             |
| R709    | `tests/test_i18n_dynamic_content_guard_r709.py`                         | Pattern C + i18n contract   | **translateDOM dynamic-content overwrite guard (prompt-wiped-to-"Loading…" root cause)**. `translateDOM()` rewrites `textContent`/placeholder of every `[data-i18n*]` element on language switch and on late `ensureDefaultLocale()` completion — wiping the rendered task prompt (`#description` ships `data-i18n="page.loading"`) permanently (the R687 render signature still matched, so polling short-circuited). Locks: both prompt render entry points strip `data-i18n` before writing real content; per-task feedback placeholder removes/restores `data-i18n-placeholder` two-way; custom-sound status and clipboard hint sync the attribute to the concrete i18n key (helper returns `{key, text}` with literal `t("…")` calls for the orphan-key scanner); `#countdown-text` carries no `data-i18n` (per-second interpolated rewrite). |
| R710    | `tests/test_stale_precompressed_fallback_r710.py`                       | Pattern A + HTTP contract   | **Stale precompressed-asset fallback guard (immutable cache poisoning root cause)**. `_send_with_optional_gzip` picked `.br`/`.gz` siblings by existence only — in the minify-without-precompress window a Brotli-capable browser received old bytes under a fresh `?v=<mtime>` URL with `Cache-Control: immutable`, pinning stale code in the cache for a year with no self-healing (bit during R709 verification). Locks the freshness rule: a compressed sibling is used only when its mtime is >= the source's; stale `.br` falls through to fresh `.gz`, and stale-everything falls back to the identity source file.                                                                                                                                                                     |

## 7. Further reading

- [`docs/lessons-learned-silent-decay.md`](lessons-learned-silent-decay.md)
  — root causes of why silent decay defeats normal review.

  release checklist; many invariants exist specifically to make
  one of those steps automated.
